Asia EM Equity Strategy - Alpha Briefing | Asia Pacific

# Macro Volatility Returns - Look for Balance Sheet Dispersion

Rate cut expectations have been delayed substantially, with Japanese FX intervention and Bank Indonesia delivering a surprise hike. We delve further into leverage and profitability and see higher gearing in LatAm, Thailand and Philippines. APAC stock level screens inside.

Top of the Agenda - Return of Macro Volatility: A further rise in Fed rate expectations remains a key challenge for Asia and EM equities. We are starting to see cracks in the previous regime of Asian central banks holding the line in anticipation of Fed cuts. In the case of Japan, a sharp JPY drop triggered intervention this week, but we think Japanese earnings and market performance trends will be driven higher by 'stronger for longer' revenue exposure to the US. The JPY remains a good deal weaker than consensus assumptions (\~140), and we see FX translation as a tailwind, with significantly more beneficiaries than indirectly negatively impacted stocks.

Alpha in Relative Leverage: A fortnight ago we highlighted the correlation breakdown between many Asia/EM markets and Fed expectations for end-2024. In this report we explore balance sheet quality and profitability as key factors likely to differentiate performance over the summer (amid negative seasonality for markets generally). At the market level, we highlight the relative strength of corporate balance sheets in Japan (OW), India (OW) and Indonesia, while noting relatively high gearing in Thailand (UW) and Philippines (EW). At the stock level, we screen for the 40 top and bottom ranked APAC companies on a composite quant measure of profitability and leverage (see Exhibit 11 ).

Exhibit 1: Median Debt/Equity Ratios - As interest rate profiles rise further, we note Asia is generally better placed than LatAm and South Africa, with relatively high gearing also seen in Thailand (UW) and Philippines (EW)

## Top of the Agenda

Daniel Blake, Kristal Ji

This edition we tackle:

• Are Corporates Geared Up for Higher Rates?

• Bullish TOPIX Trend Not Undercut by JPY Depreciation and Intervention

## Are Corporates Geared Up for Higher Rates?

## Major shift in policy outlook

Here we look at the state of corporate balance sheets amid an ongoing shift up in rate expectations (and a surprise Bank of Indonesia hike), following last fortnight's look at shifting correlations to higher Fed funds expectations (see Alpha, Beta, Thematic Biweekly – Navigating Higher Rates, Elections and Geopolitics, 16 Apr 2024).

In general we see Asia-ex-Japan and EM central banks likely to follow the lead of the Fed and their DM peers in cutting rates, and the breadth of repricing of G9 expectations should see investors at least stress test balance sheets for a higher for longer environment.

For instance, market expectations for the timing of RBA cuts have moved out by almost a year. While in India, over the last 3 months the timing of a first RBI cut has moved from June 2024 to now be pricing \~18bp worth of a potential hike. Expectations for RBI policy rates to be a full 25bp lower than current levels (6.5%) have been delayed until \~August 2025.

Exhibit 2: Market-implied timing of first 25bp rate cut - Material changes for the Fed and RBA
![](images/9496d2b02166e6f4950f62a41155c0bcc66f73ee2434799d474a9dc04422170a.jpg)
Source: Bloomberg, Morgan Stanley Research

## Corporate gearing looks manageable, but relatively higher in LatAm, Thailand and Philippines

In terms of overall gearing levels, we see Japan and EM Asia as the most conservative, while Latin American companies are carrying the most gearing on a net debt to equity basis. Within ASEAN we also see clear differences between low-gearing levels in Indonesia and highly-geared balance sheets in Thailand and the Philippines.

Exhibit 3: Total Debt to Equity by Region
![](images/b1de4c919f9b8cb7b2052140548794dc68e1a615acd450c4c602f6b612c0eda6.jpg)
Source: Factset, Morgan Stanley Research. All values are market cap weighted medians. Data as on Apri 30, 2024

Exhibit 4: Net Debt to EBITDA by Region
![](images/17d8667ba6705b180e3aba681d8532c63590799122c7a62de586fc57d138bef5.jpg)
Source: Factset, Morgan Stanley Research. All values are market cap weighted medians. Data as on Apri 30, 2024

Exhibit 5: Total Debt to Equity - ASEAN
![](images/53f67fe78fd2bb1ed772851aaee32dd0b4787bd6479272280c44fbba06a07d48.jpg)
Source: Factset, Morgan Stanley Research. All values are market cap weighted medians. Data as on Apri 30, 2024

Exhibit 6: Net Debt to EBITDA - ASEAN
![](images/e56a370e6e5298c92dbd98eb3ead5ef974fdcad68be471db5e5d7cc555a40994.jpg)
Source: Factset, Morgan Stanley Research. All values are market cap weighted medians. Data as on Apri 30, 2024

Taking into account the relatively high average interest rates, we see EBIT interest coverage ratios particularly low for South America (median 5.9x), while Asia is also relatively low (11x). On this metric, Japan clearly stands out with a median 19.2x interest coverage, consistent with our view that Japanese companies can and should be taking on more gearing and making better use of many net cash balance sheets to improve ROE and reduce their cost of capital.

Exhibit 7: EBIT Interest Coverage by Region
![](images/a4b2263009ddcdf11573ae8b3c3e81d9dbdca640dcc94fca9bb6b0f6459b5713.jpg)
Source: Factset, Morgan Stanley Research. All values are market cap weighted medians. Data as on Apri 30, 2024

Exhibit 8: EBIT Interest Coverage - ASEAN
![](images/791062a70b09b61a21f8716358414f98c917dd9cc9e0360b6d5b14009d37210b.jpg)
Source: Factset, Morgan Stanley Research. All values are market cap weighted medians. Data as on Apri 30, 2024

In terms of portfolio positioning, we recommend positioning up in quality when it comes to profitability and leverage, to better mitigate risks from rising interest expense or a

potentially dilutive capital raising or restructuring. Over the last 6 weeks, we are starting to see the equity market rewarding companies with low-leverage, while profitability as a factor has been outperforming since 3Q23.

Exhibit 9: Asia-ex-Japan Quality Factor Performance - Overall Composite has been strong since 2023, with Profitability outperforming since 3Q23 and Low Leverage now picking up YTD
![](images/963eeedf1375e63aa7387106fe78bcf6b31c1b359f6fff2c70e2c424cf4c9960.jpg)
Source: FactSet, Morgan Stanley Research

## Bullish TOPIX Trend Not Undercut by JPY Depreciation and Intervention

Japanese equities have plateaued recently amid a momentum factor reversal and seasonal weakness in April, which typically follows the fiscal year-end, with companies also going ex-dividend. Some investors have also raised concerns that the further weakening in the

JPY has perhaps turned FX depreciation into a headwind, rather than a tailwind even for the JPY-denominated indices.

Our view is that the stronger-for-longer US macro outlook remains fundamentally positive for Japanese revenues and earnings, with the translation impact of a weaker JPY still a clear tailwind. Our latest Global Exposure Guide showed Japanese companies under coverage generating almost 20% of their total revenues in the US and 47% of total revenues abroad. The direct impact of JPY through operating margins and translation is positive for the majority of these companies, with only a fraction of the index likely to be directly negatively impacted. Across the index, we last estimated that every 1 USD/JPY weakness leading to 0.5% expansion in overall Japan operating income.

Exhibit 13: TOPIX consensus EPS trend with MS forecasts – We see high single-digit growth expectations as achievable in our base case for global soft landing and strong nominal Japanese GDP growth
![](images/06a6e3fc764d074b576a38d54f546e8f30a3e1703539ef6dcff9762aab526c0f.jpg)
Source: IBES, Datastream, Morgan Stanley Research, Data as on April 18, 2024

The indirect impacts of a weaker JPY need to be monitored, with the key channels being through a negative impact on investor confidence and risk premia, or through a faster pass-through of higher global rates into the BoJ outlook. On this front, following our recent Fed forecast adjustments, our economists have added one more hike to the BoJ outlook. They now see a 15bp hike in July and a subsequent 25bp hike to 50bp in January 2025. This is based on our outlook for Japan’s inflation gradually stabilizing to levels at slightly below 2% in 2025.

In our view, signs of a crisis of investor confidence are limited - with domestic equity inflows picking up in recent weeks (assisted by NISA accounts), which is potentially catalysing foreign outflows (for every buyer a seller). And the overall equity market impact of higher rates should be limited while real rates remain materially negative, with our economists seeing neutral nominal interest rates closer to 1%.

As a result, we expect Japan equities to continue performing well, especially in JPY terms. We see solid progress on the corporate reform and domestic reflation thematics, driving ongoing positive earnings revisions ( Exhibit 13 ), while valuations remain reasonable (12mf

P/E at 14.9x vs our target of 15.0x).

## Flows and Positioning

## Kristal Ji

• Aggregate flows (EPFR, to April 24, 2024): EM and China recorded outflows, and active long-only US and EU funds were still selling Chinese equities ( Exhibit 48 ).

• Flows by market (EPFR, to April 24, 2024): Relative to AUM, Taiwan was the only market reporting inflows within EM, while Philippines, Indonesia and Saudi Arabia recorded the largest outflows. ( Exhibit 47 ).

• Japan flows (JPX, to April 19, 2024): Japan foreign investors sold off during the week of April 13-19 (net selling US\$7.3bn), while inflows from retail investors were substantial (US\$7.2bn) who have net bought US\$6.1bn YTD 2024 ( Exhibit 49 and Exhibit 50 ).

• Asia/EM Positioning (EPFR, to March 2024): India underweight in GEM funds allocation has been widening given managers have not yet caught up with the rising index weight. China is seeing dropping benchmark weight and GEM funds managers' allocation, with a smaller underweight of 69bp underweight vs. benchmark. Japan underweight in global long-only equity funds widened to 0.59% as of end-March, though we continue to expect Japan to eventually come to overweight ( Exhibit 52 ).

## Appendix - APAC Aerospace & Defence Stock List

We have identified a universe of listed securities across Asia Pacific markets with Aerospace & Defense exposure. Our approach is not exhaustive and may capture businesses with only a minority exposure to the sector. We utilised a combination of the following screens:

1. Companies with GICS Industry or BICS Industry Name classified as "Aerospace & Defense".

2. Companies with more than 5% of revenue from FactSet RBICS sub-sector “Aerospace and Defense Manufacturing”.

3. Companies' Business Descriptions with Aerospace & Defense-related key words found.

4. Japan's Ministry of Defense – Top companies in the annual procurement list.

5. Korea's Ministry of Trade, Industry and Energy – Companies designated as defense contractors.

## STOCK RATINGS

Morgan Stanley uses a relative rating system using terms such as Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated or Underweight (see definitions below). Morgan Stanley does not assign ratings of Buy, Hold or Sell to the stocks we cover. Overweight, Equal-weight, Not-Rated and Underweight are not the equivalent of buy, hold and sell. Investors should carefully read the definitions of al ratings used in Morgan Stanley Research. In addition, since Morgan Stanley Research contains more complete information concerning the analyst's views, investors should carefully read Morgan Stanley Research, in its entirety, and not infer the contents from the rating alone. In any case, ratings (or research) should not be used or relied upon as investment advice. An investor's decision to buy or sell a stock should depend on individual circumstances (such as the investor's existing holdings) and other considerations.

## Analyst Stock Ratings

Overweight (O). The stock's total return is expected to exceed the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Equal-weight (E). The stock's total return is expected to be in line with the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Not-Rated (NR). Currently the analyst does not have adequate conviction about the stock's total return relative to the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Underweight (U). The stock's total return is expected to be below the average total return of the analyst's industry (or industry team's) coverage universe, on a risk-adjusted basis, over the next 12-18 months.

Unless otherwise specified, the time frame for price targets included in Morgan Stanley Research is 12 to 18 months.

## Analyst Industry Views

Attractive (A): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be attractive vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below.

In-Line (I): The analyst expects the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months to be in line with the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Cautious (C): The analyst views the performance of his or her industry coverage universe over the next 12-18 months with caution vs. the relevant broad market benchmark, as indicated below. Benchmarks for each region are as follows: North America - S&P 500; Latin America - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI Latin America Index; Europe - MSCI Europe; Japan - TOPIX; Asia - relevant MSCI country index or MSCI sub-regional index or MSCI AC Asia Pacific ex Japan Index.
